# Chapter 18: Social Capital, Mutual Aid and Governance

*Theme: social networks, public institutions and the politics of protection*

In the early hours of 15 July 2021, heavy rain fell on the Ahr valley in western Germany. Between 100 and 200 millimetres fell in less than 24 hours on ground that was already wet, and the small river rose within hours from a quiet stream to a torrent. In the district of Ahrweiler, 134 people died; across Germany and Belgium, more than 180 died, and damage reached tens of billions of euros.

The warning chain had partially worked. The German meteorological service issued warnings, some local authorities evacuated, and some residents left. But in many places the warnings did not translate into action. People did not believe the river could rise that fast; some local officials did not order evacuation; and Germany had no nationwide system for sending alerts directly to mobile phones, a gap that was closed only later.

The Ahr valley flood is a case about governance rather than meteorology. The rainfall was extreme but not unimaginable; models had warned that such events were becoming more likely. What failed was the chain that connects a forecast to a decision, a decision to a warning, and a warning to a household that acts. Every link in that chain is an institution, and institutions can be designed well or badly.

This chapter examines the social and political infrastructure of survival: the networks that help neighbours, the institutions that issue warnings and organise response, and the governance arrangements that determine whether protection reaches the people who need it. It argues that social capital and public institutions are not alternatives but complements, and that the quality of governance is one of the strongest predictors of how many people die when hazard strikes.

## 18.1 Social Capital as a Survival Resource

Social capital refers to the networks, norms and trust that enable people to act together. It is not a metaphor: it can be measured, and it predicts outcomes. Sociologists distinguish bonding capital, which links people who are similar and close, bridging capital, which links people across different groups, and linking capital, which connects citizens to institutions with power. Each plays a different role in a disaster.

Bonding capital provides immediate help: the neighbour who checks on an elderly resident, the family that shares water, the congregation that opens its hall. Bridging capital provides access to resources outside the immediate circle: the community group that knows how to apply for aid, the network that can mobilise volunteers. Linking capital determines whether a community can influence the institutions that decide where the levee is built and who receives compensation.

Daniel Aldrich, in a series of studies of disaster recovery in Japan, India and the United States, found that social capital predicted recovery better than income, damage or government spending. Communities with dense networks recovered faster because they could share information, allocate resources and hold institutions accountable. The finding is important because it suggests that resilience is not only bought with money; it is also built through relationships.

## 18.2 The Evidence: Neighbourhoods and Survival

The classic study of social capital and heat is Eric Klinenberg's analysis of the 1995 Chicago heatwave, which killed more than 700 people in a week. Klinenberg compared two neighbourhoods with similar demographics and found that death rates differed sharply. In the mostly Latino Little Village, dense social networks, active street life and functioning institutions protected residents; in North Lawndale, depopulation, abandoned buildings and fear kept people isolated and indoors.

The mechanism was social, not physiological. Elderly people in Little Village were visited, were known to their neighbours, and went outside; elderly people in North Lawndale were afraid to open their doors and had no one to check on them. Air conditioning and income mattered, but they did not explain the gap. What explained it was whether people were connected to others who would notice their absence.

Similar findings appear in studies of floods, hurricanes and wildfires. Social isolation is consistently associated with higher mortality, and neighbourhood cohesion with lower mortality. The implication for policy is that programmes which build connection — community centres, neighbourhood associations, volunteer networks, outreach to isolated residents — are forms of disaster preparedness, even when they are not framed that way.

## 18.3 Mutual Aid and Volunteer Response

When formal systems are overwhelmed, people organise. After Hurricane Katrina, the Cajun Navy — a loose network of volunteers with boats — rescued thousands of people from flooded neighbourhoods. After Hurricane Sandy, Occupy Sandy organised volunteers to deliver supplies to areas that official relief had not reached. After the 2017 Mexico City earthquake, civilian brigades known as topos dug survivors out of rubble before professional teams arrived.

Mutual aid is most effective when it is connected to formal systems rather than replacing them. Volunteers know local conditions and can move faster, but they lack equipment, training and coordination capacity. The best outcomes occur when formal agencies create spaces for volunteers, provide information and logistics, and avoid treating them as a threat to command and control.

The Kerala floods of 2018 illustrate the point. The state government mobilised fishing communities, whose boats and knowledge of local waters were essential for rescuing people from flooded areas. The collaboration worked because the government recognised the fishers as partners and provided fuel, coordination and information. It was not spontaneous harmony; it was organised cooperation.

## 18.4 The Warning Chain and the Last Mile

A warning is a chain of translations: from observation to forecast, from forecast to decision, from decision to message, from message to household, and from household to action. Each link can fail, and the failure is usually invisible until the event. The Ahr valley flood failed at several links: forecasts were available but not understood, decisions were delayed, and messages did not reach everyone.

The last mile is the hardest. Even when a warning is issued, it may not reach people who are asleep, who do not speak the language, who have no phone, or who distrust the messenger. Effective systems use multiple channels — sirens, cell broadcast, radio, social media, door-to-door — and repeat the message in plain language with specific instructions. A warning that says a river will flood is less useful than one that says leave this street now and go to this location.

Trust determines whether the message is believed. Communities that have experienced accurate warnings in the past are more likely to respond to the next one; communities that have been ignored or misled are less likely. Building trust requires consistency, transparency about uncertainty, and visible follow-through. It is slow to build and fast to lose, which is why it belongs in the same category as physical infrastructure.

## 18.5 Institutions, Trust and Risk Governance

The quality of government is one of the strongest determinants of disaster outcomes. Countries with effective bureaucracies, well-funded emergency services and honest procurement suffer fewer deaths and recover faster than countries with the same hazard exposure and weaker institutions. The contrast between the 2010 earthquakes in Haiti and Chile is often cited: Haiti's magnitude 7.0 earthquake killed more than 200,000 people, while Chile's magnitude 8.8 earthquake three weeks later killed about 500, largely because of building standards, preparedness and response capacity.

Trust matters because compliance depends on it. People who trust official warnings are more likely to evacuate, to accept restrictions and to cooperate with relief. Trust is built through competence, honesty and fairness, and it is damaged by failures, contradictions and perceived favouritism. The pandemic illustrated how quickly trust can erode when messaging is inconsistent, and how costly that erosion is.

Risk governance requires coordination across levels and sectors. A heat plan involves health, meteorology, social services, utilities and civil society; a flood plan involves water management, land use, emergency services and infrastructure. The failure mode is fragmentation, where each agency optimises its own mandate and no one is accountable for the whole. Effective governance assigns clear responsibility, provides resources, and requires coordination.

## 18.6 Decentralisation and Local Capacity

Much of the work of disaster response happens at the local level, where knowledge of terrain, population and relationships is greatest. Decentralisation can improve responsiveness, but only if local governments have the resources, skills and accountability to act. In many countries, decentralisation has transferred responsibilities without transferring revenue, leaving municipalities with mandates they cannot fund.

Indonesia's village law, which gave villages substantial budgets and authority, is one example of an attempt to build local capacity. India's panchayat system and the Philippines' barangay structure serve similar functions. Where local institutions are well functioning, they can organise evacuation, maintain drainage and manage relief; where they are captured by elites or starved of funds, they can exclude the people who most need help.

The design question is how to build local capacity without creating local capture. That requires transparent budgets, participatory planning, external audit and support from higher levels of government. It also requires time, because institutions develop through practice. A decentralisation that begins five years before a disaster is unlikely to produce capable institutions by the time it arrives.

## 18.7 Corruption, Capture and Reconstruction

Disasters create large flows of money, and large flows of money create opportunities for corruption. Reconstruction contracts, relief distribution and land deals are all vulnerable. In some cases, the result is that aid does not reach survivors; in others, it reaches them in inferior form, with buildings that fail in the next event.

Elite capture is a subtler problem. Relief and reconstruction can reinforce existing inequalities if the people who control distribution also control access to information, land and credit. Post-disaster land grabs have been documented in several countries, particularly where tenure was informal before the event. Women, renters and informal residents are often the most exposed to these dynamics.

The antidote is transparency. Publishing beneficiary lists, contract awards and spending data allows communities and journalists to hold institutions accountable. Independent audits, community monitoring and grievance mechanisms can catch problems early. None of these is sufficient alone, but together they make corruption more difficult and more visible, which is often enough to change behaviour.

## 18.8 Disaster Risk Finance and Accountability

Money is decisive in the aftermath of a disaster, and the speed with which it arrives determines whether households recover or fall into debt. Traditional financing relies on post-disaster appeals, which are slow and uncertain. Pre-arranged finance — contingent credit, insurance, catastrophe bonds, regional risk pools — can disburse quickly and predictably.

The Caribbean Catastrophe Risk Insurance Facility, established in 2007, is an example of a regional pool that pays out quickly after a qualifying event. African Risk Capacity and the Pacific Catastrophe Risk Assessment and Financing Initiative serve similar functions. These instruments are not a substitute for national investment in resilience, but they reduce the fiscal shock of a disaster and allow governments to respond without diverting budgets from development.

Accountability is the other side of finance. Governments that receive adaptation and relief funds should be required to report how they were spent, and parliaments, audit institutions and civil society should have the capacity to scrutinise. Without accountability, finance can be absorbed without reducing risk, which is one reason why the correlation between aid received and resilience achieved is weaker than it should be.

## 18.9 Regional and International Cooperation

Climate hazards do not respect borders. Storms cross coastlines, rivers flow across countries, and displacement spills over frontiers. Regional cooperation can improve early warning, share resources and coordinate response. The ASEAN Coordinating Centre for Humanitarian Assistance on Disaster Management (AHA Centre) is an example: it maintains stockpiles, coordinates regional response and supports member states during emergencies.

International cooperation is most effective when it supports local institutions rather than substituting for them. Externally managed responses often arrive late, cost more, and leave little capacity behind. The Grand Bargain, a set of commitments to improve humanitarian financing, emphasises localisation for this reason, though progress has been slow.

Climate finance is also a form of governance. The commitment by wealthy countries to provide US$100 billion a year in climate finance has been met late and unevenly, and the adaptation share remains far below what vulnerable countries need. The gap is not only a matter of money; it is a matter of trust, because countries that promised and did not deliver have less credibility when they ask for cooperation on emissions.

## 18.10 Polycentric Governance and Learning

No single institution can manage climate risk. The most effective systems are polycentric: many centres of decision-making, connected by rules and information, each able to act within its domain. Elinor Ostrom's work on governing the commons showed that communities can manage shared resources effectively when they have clear boundaries, rules that fit local conditions, and mechanisms for collective choice.

Polycentric systems are resilient because they do not depend on a single point of failure. If a national agency is slow, a city can act; if a city is captured, a community group can organise. But polycentricity is not anarchy: it requires coordination, shared information and accountability. The challenge is to design systems that allow local initiative while ensuring that the most vulnerable are not left out.

Learning is the final requirement. Institutions that treat each disaster as a lesson, investigate what happened, and change their procedures are more resilient than those that do not. The inquiry into the Ahr valley flood led to the introduction of cell broadcast warnings in Germany; the inquiry into Hurricane Katrina led to reforms in emergency management. Learning requires the political willingness to accept criticism, which is often in short supply when blame is at stake.

## 18.11 Case Study: The Ahr Valley Flood, Germany 2021

On 14 and 15 July 2021, a slow-moving low-pressure system produced extreme rainfall over western Germany, Belgium and the Netherlands. In the Ahr valley, up to 200 millimetres fell in less than 24 hours, and the river rose within hours. The flood destroyed houses, roads and bridges, cut power and communications, and killed 134 people in the Ahrweiler district alone; across Germany and Belgium the death toll exceeded 180, and damage was estimated at more than €30 billion.

The meteorological service had issued warnings, and some local authorities acted. But many people did not evacuate, partly because they did not believe the river could rise so fast, partly because the warnings were not specific enough, and partly because no mechanism existed to push an alert directly to every phone in the affected area. Germany introduced cell broadcast warnings only in 2023, after the event.

The failure was distributed across the chain. Forecasts were available but not translated into clear local instructions; some officials delayed evacuation because they feared false alarms; and communication systems failed when infrastructure was destroyed. The result was a disaster that was worse than the rainfall alone would explain, which is the definition of a governance failure in disaster risk.

The response after the event was substantial. Investigations were commissioned, responsibilities were clarified, and warning systems were improved. The flood became a reference point for German disaster policy, and it changed public expectations about warning and preparedness. The lesson was not that Germany lacked capacity; it was that capacity must be organised into a chain that reaches the last household.

For other countries, the lessons are specific. Warnings must be specific and actionable, not merely meteorological. Multiple channels must be used, including ones that do not depend on electricity or internet. Local officials need clear authority and training to order evacuation. And the public must be educated about what warnings mean, so that a warning is not dismissed as routine. Each of these is a governance task, not a technological one.

## 18.12 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Governance in 2050

The historical record shows that governance quality explains much of the variation in disaster mortality. Countries with strong institutions have reduced deaths from comparable hazards over the past century, while countries with weak institutions have not. The trend is not automatic: it depends on investment, accountability and learning.

Present conditions set the baseline. Trust in government has declined in many democracies, while the demands on government have increased. Local governments, which do most of the operational work, are often underfunded. International climate finance has fallen short of commitments, and adaptation finance reaches a small fraction of what is needed. Meanwhile, the hazards are becoming more severe and more frequent.

The assumptions behind the projections are the trajectory of institutional quality, the level of finance, and the degree of international cooperation. A world that invests in local capacity, transparency and regional cooperation can manage rising risk; a world that does not will see disasters become political crises. The table summarises the range.

[[TABLE:Table 18.1 Governance indicators, 2050]]
| Institution | Core function | Common failure | Reform | Indicator |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Local government | Evacuation; shelters | Underfunded; unclear authority | Devolved finance; training | Response time |
| Warning systems | Forecast to household | Last-mile gap | Multi-channel; cell broadcast | Reach and compliance |
| Emergency services | Rescue; coordination | Fragmentation | Joint command; exercises | Coverage |
| Social protection | Cash; food; shelter | Exclusion errors | Registries; portable benefits | Coverage of poor |
| Anti-corruption | Procurement integrity | Elite capture | Open contracts; audits | Audit findings |
| Regional bodies | Shared stockpiles | Slow coordination | Pre-agreed protocols | Response time |

Two conclusions follow. First, governance is a determinant of survival, not a background condition; the difference between a well-organised response and a disorganised one is measured in lives. Second, the reforms that matter most are unglamorous: clear authority, adequate local finance, transparent procurement, and practised procedures.

What would change these conclusions? A sustained investment in local government capacity would improve response even without new technology. Universal social protection would reduce the exclusion that leaves the poorest without help. And stronger regional cooperation could compensate for weak national capacity in small states. The most important variable remains the pace of climate change, which determines how often institutions will be tested, and whether they have time to learn between tests.

# Chapter 19: Renewable Energy and Appropriate Technologies

*Theme: decentralised power, storage and the economics of clean energy*

In the chars of northern Bangladesh — islands of silt that appear and disappear with the rivers — grid electricity arrived late, if at all. Households relied on kerosene lamps for light, which produced poor illumination, indoor smoke and a recurring expense that consumed a significant share of household income. Children studied under flickering light; shops closed at dusk; and clinics could not refrigerate vaccines.

Beginning in the late 1990s, a programme run by the Infrastructure Development Company Limited and supported by the World Bank and other donors began financing solar home systems through microcredit. Households paid a small down payment and instalments over two to three years. By the early 2020s, more than four million systems had been installed, reaching an estimated twenty million people, and Bangladesh had one of the largest off-grid solar programmes in the world.

The results were measurable. Kerosene use fell; children gained study hours; shops extended opening; and households reported better air quality and fewer burns. The systems were small — typically 50 to 100 watts — and they did not power industry. But they demonstrated something important: a modular technology, financed appropriately, could deliver a basic service to people whom the grid had not reached and would not reach for years.

This chapter examines renewable energy and appropriate technologies as instruments of resilience. It covers solar and wind, storage and grid flexibility, mini-grids and off-grid systems, clean cooking, efficiency, and the supply chains and politics that determine whether these technologies spread fast enough. The argument is not that technology alone will solve the climate crisis, but that the falling cost of modular clean energy has changed what is possible for households, communities and countries.

## 19.1 Why Renewables Are a Resilience Technology

Renewable energy is usually discussed as a mitigation strategy — a way to reduce emissions. It is also an adaptation strategy, because it changes the failure modes of energy systems. A coal plant depends on a fuel supply chain, cooling water and a centralised grid; a solar array depends on sunlight. When floods block roads or droughts reduce river flows, the solar array keeps producing, and a system built from many small units fails gradually rather than all at once.

Modularity is the key property. A large central plant is efficient but brittle: one failure can remove a gigawatt from the grid. A distributed system composed of thousands of rooftops, batteries and small generators can lose pieces without losing the whole. That is why hospitals, clinics and community shelters increasingly combine grid connection with rooftop solar and batteries, so that they can operate when the grid fails.

Renewables also reduce exposure to fuel price shocks. Countries that import oil and gas are vulnerable to price spikes caused by conflict, shipping disruption or market manipulation; the 2022 energy crisis, following Russia's invasion of Ukraine, raised electricity and heating costs across Europe and pushed millions of households into fuel poverty. Solar and wind, once built, have no fuel cost, which makes their operating economics predictable even when commodity markets are not.

## 19.2 Solar: The Learning Curve

Solar photovoltaic modules have followed the steepest cost decline of any energy technology in history. The price of a module fell by roughly 90 per cent between 2010 and 2023, from more than a dollar per watt to around twenty cents, and installed system costs fell almost as fast. The decline was driven by manufacturing scale, learning by doing, and competition, and it transformed solar from a niche technology into the cheapest source of new electricity in many markets.

Deployment followed the price. Global solar capacity passed one terawatt in 2022 and continued to grow at record rates, with annual additions exceeding 400 gigawatts by 2023 and continuing to rise. China accounted for the largest share of both manufacturing and installation, but growth spread across India, the United States, Europe, Brazil and the Middle East. In many countries, solar now supplies a significant share of daytime electricity.

The technology has limits. Solar produces only during daylight, so it requires storage or complementary generation to meet evening demand; output varies with weather and season; and it requires land or roof space. In high-latitude winters, output can fall sharply. These limits are manageable with storage, transmission and diversified generation, but they mean that solar alone is not a complete system. The integration challenge, not the generation cost, is now the main constraint.

## 19.3 Wind Power and the Variability Problem

Wind power is the other mature renewable technology. Global capacity passed 1,000 gigawatts in 2023, with the largest installations in China, the United States, Germany, India and Spain. Offshore wind has grown rapidly because turbines can be larger and winds steadier at sea, and because offshore sites are close to coastal demand centres. The cost of offshore wind fell sharply before rising again in 2022–2023 as supply chains tightened and interest rates rose.

Wind shares solar's variability but not its daily pattern. In some regions, wind output is higher at night and in winter, which complements solar; in others, wind droughts — periods of unusually low wind — can last for days or weeks. The 2021 wind drought in Europe reduced output at a time of high demand, contributing to the energy price spike. Systems that rely heavily on wind need storage, interconnection and dispatchable backup to manage these periods.

Wind also has social constraints. Turbines are visible, can be noisy, and affect landscapes and wildlife; opposition has slowed projects in several countries. The constraints are real but manageable with better siting, community benefit sharing and early consultation. The alternative — continuing to rely on fossil fuels — carries larger environmental and social costs, but that comparison is not always made in local planning debates.

## 19.4 Storage, Flexibility and the Grid

The central challenge of a renewable-heavy grid is matching supply to demand. Electricity cannot be stored cheaply in large quantities for long periods, so system operators must balance generation and consumption in real time. Batteries, pumped hydro, demand response and interconnection are the main tools for doing so.

Lithium-ion battery costs fell by about 85 per cent between 2010 and 2023, and grid-scale storage grew rapidly, from a few gigawatts to tens of gigawatts globally. Batteries are well suited to shifting solar output from midday to evening and to providing fast frequency response, but they are less suited to storing energy across seasons. Pumped hydro, which stores energy as water in an upper reservoir, remains the largest form of grid storage and can provide long-duration capacity where geography allows.

Flexibility also comes from how demand is managed. Time-of-use tariffs, smart thermostats, industrial load shifting and electric vehicle charging can move consumption to times when supply is abundant. Interconnection allows regions with different weather to share power, which reduces the total storage needed. The combination of storage, demand flexibility, interconnection and diversified generation is what makes a high-renewable grid reliable, not any single technology.

## 19.5 Mini-Grids and Off-Grid Solar

About 700 million people still lack access to electricity, most of them in sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia. Extending the grid to remote areas is expensive and slow, and in some places it may never be economic. Mini-grids — small, local generation and distribution systems, often solar with battery storage — can provide reliable power to communities that the grid does not reach.

The economics have improved dramatically. A solar mini-grid can now deliver electricity at a cost competitive with diesel in many off-grid areas, and far below the cost of kerosene or candles per unit of light. The main barriers are financing, regulation and maintenance: mini-grids require upfront capital, clear rules on tariffs and grid arrival, and local capacity to operate and repair equipment.

The Bangladesh solar home system programme illustrates both the potential and the limits of off-grid approaches. It reached millions of households with basic lighting and phone charging, but the systems were too small for productive use, and as the grid expanded, some became redundant. The next generation of programmes combines larger systems, appliance financing and productive uses — irrigation pumps, cold storage, milling — so that electricity generates income rather than only light.

## 19.6 Clean Cooking and Household Energy

Around 2.3 billion people still cook with solid fuels — wood, charcoal, dung, crop residue — in open fires or inefficient stoves. The resulting household air pollution is estimated to cause 3.7 million premature deaths a year, mostly among women and children, from respiratory disease, heart disease and stroke. It is one of the largest environmental health burdens in the world, and it is concentrated among the poorest households.

The transition to clean cooking requires more than a stove. Liquefied petroleum gas requires reliable supply and affordable cylinders; electricity requires reliable power and suitable cookers; improved biomass stoves require fuel processing and maintenance. Households often use a combination of fuels — a practice called stove stacking — because no single option meets all needs. Programmes that assume a single switch tend to fail.

The health and climate benefits are substantial. Clean cooking reduces indoor air pollution, saves time spent collecting fuel, reduces deforestation where wood is harvested unsustainably, and cuts black carbon emissions. The barriers are economic and logistical rather than technical, which is why progress has been slow despite decades of effort and why sustained subsidy, supply chain development and behaviour change are all required.

## 19.7 Appropriate Technology and Local Capacity

Appropriate technology is technology that fits the context: affordable, repairable, maintainable with local skills, and suited to local conditions. A solar home system that can be repaired by a local technician is more useful than a more efficient system that cannot be fixed. A water pump that can be maintained by the community is more sustainable than a complex system that requires external specialists.

The concept has been criticised as condescending when it implies that poor communities should accept lower technology. The critique is fair when appropriate technology means second-best; it is not fair when it means fit for purpose. The relevant question is not whether a technology is advanced but whether it can be sustained, repaired and afforded over its lifetime, and whether it meets the needs of the people who use it.

Local manufacturing and assembly can improve sustainability by creating jobs, reducing dependence on imports and building repair capacity. Several countries have used industrial policy to develop solar manufacturing, battery assembly and appliance production, with varying success. The most effective programmes combine technology transfer with training and finance, so that local firms can maintain and adapt the equipment rather than merely sell it.

## 19.8 Critical Minerals and Supply Chains

Clean energy technologies require minerals: lithium, cobalt, nickel, graphite and manganese for batteries; copper and aluminium for grids; rare earths for magnets; and silver and silicon for solar cells. The International Energy Agency estimates that demand for critical minerals could triple by 2030 under announced pledges, and that supply may not keep pace without new investment.

Supply is concentrated. China refines a large share of the world's lithium, cobalt and graphite, and dominates solar manufacturing; the Democratic Republic of the Congo supplies most of the world's cobalt; and Indonesia is a major source of nickel. Concentration creates resilience risks, because a single disruption — a export restriction, a conflict, a natural disaster — can affect global supply.

Diversification, recycling and substitution can reduce the risk. Recycling recovers lithium, cobalt and nickel from batteries and can supply a growing share of demand as the stock of deployed batteries increases. Substitution — sodium-ion batteries, iron-based chemistries, rare-earth-free motors — can reduce dependence on constrained materials. Mining also carries social and environmental costs, including water use, land conflict and labour abuses, which must be addressed if the transition is to be just.

## 19.9 Efficiency and Demand

The cheapest unit of energy is the one that is not used. Energy efficiency — better insulation, more efficient appliances, LED lighting, heat pumps, efficient motors — reduces demand, lowers bills and cuts emissions, often at negative cost. The International Energy Agency has repeatedly found that efficiency measures could deliver a large share of the emissions reductions needed by 2030, and that many are cheaper than new supply.

Cooling is a critical case. Demand for air conditioning is growing rapidly in hot countries as incomes rise and temperatures increase, and it could become one of the largest sources of electricity demand. Efficient air conditioners, better building design, shading and passive cooling can reduce that demand substantially. The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, which phases down hydrofluorocarbons, is expected to avoid significant warming while improving appliance efficiency.

Efficiency policy faces a paradox: it is invisible, distributed and often requires upfront investment for savings that accrue over years. Households and firms systematically underinvest in efficiency because of split incentives — the landlord pays for insulation while the tenant pays the bills — and because of limited attention and capital. Standards, labelling, financing and technical support can overcome these barriers, and they are among the most cost-effective climate policies available.

## 19.10 The Political Economy of Transition

Energy transitions are political. Fossil fuel subsidies, which totalled more than a trillion dollars in 2022 during the price crisis, distort markets in favour of incumbents. Grid planning, permitting and market rules determine whether renewables can connect. Utility business models designed for centralised generation can resist distributed production. And communities that depend on fossil fuel employment resist changes that threaten their livelihoods.

A just transition requires addressing those interests rather than ignoring them. That means retraining and income support for workers, economic diversification for coal-dependent regions, and consultation with communities affected by new infrastructure. It also means ensuring that the benefits of the transition — cheaper electricity, cleaner air, new jobs — reach the people who bear its costs.

The pace of transition is now the central variable. Renewables are cheaper than new fossil fuel plants in most markets, and the constraints are increasingly grid capacity, permitting, storage and political will rather than technology cost. The countries that build quickly will benefit from lower costs and greater energy security; the countries that delay will face higher costs, stranded assets and greater exposure to fuel price shocks.

## 19.11 Case Study: Bangladesh's Solar Home System Programme

In the 1990s, most rural Bangladeshi households had no electricity. Kerosene was the main source of light, and the cost was significant for poor families. The grid reached urban areas and some rural towns, but extending it to remote chars and villages was expensive and slow. The government, with support from the World Bank and other donors, established a programme to finance solar home systems through microcredit institutions.

The model was simple. A household paid a small down payment, typically 10 to 15 per cent of the system cost, and the remainder in instalments over two to three years, financed by microcredit. The systems were small — 50 to 100 watts — sufficient for lights, a phone charger and a small television. Local technicians were trained to install and maintain them, and quality standards were enforced through testing and certification.

The programme grew rapidly. By the early 2020s, more than four million systems had been installed, reaching an estimated twenty million people. Evaluations found that kerosene use fell, study hours increased, shops extended opening hours, and households reported better air quality and fewer burns. The programme also created a network of local technicians and businesses, which built capacity for later renewable energy development.

The limitations were equally instructive. The systems were too small for productive uses such as irrigation, milling or refrigeration, so they did not generate income. As the grid expanded, some households switched to grid electricity and abandoned their systems. And the financing model depended on microcredit, which excluded the poorest households who could not afford the down payment or the instalments.

The lessons for other countries are specific. Off-grid solar can deliver basic services quickly and at scale, but it needs quality standards, local maintenance capacity and financing that reaches the poorest. It works best as a complement to grid expansion rather than a permanent substitute, and it should be designed to support productive use, not only lighting. Bangladesh's programme was not a complete solution, but it demonstrated that modular technology, appropriate finance and local institutions can change millions of lives.

## 19.12 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Energy in 2050

The historical record shows a rapid shift. Renewables supplied about 30 per cent of global electricity in 2023, up from about 20 per cent a decade earlier, and solar and wind accounted for most new capacity additions. Costs fell faster than almost any forecast anticipated, and deployment repeatedly exceeded projections. The trend is now driven by economics as much as by policy.

Present conditions set the baseline. About 700 million people lack electricity and 2.3 billion lack clean cooking, while global energy demand continues to rise. Fossil fuels still supply about 80 per cent of primary energy, and subsidies continue to favour them. Grids, storage and permitting are the binding constraints in many countries, and critical mineral supply chains are concentrated.

The assumptions behind the projections are the pace of cost decline, the rate of grid investment, and the strength of policy. A world that invests in grids, storage and manufacturing can reach a predominantly renewable electricity system by 2050; a world that does not will continue to depend on fossil fuels and face higher costs and greater volatility. The table summarises the range.

[[TABLE:Table 19.1 Clean energy indicators, 2050]]
| Technology | Cost trend | Best use | Constraint | Resilience benefit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Solar PV | −90% since 2010 | Daytime power; rooftops | Land; night output | Modular; no fuel |
| Wind | Falling; volatile | Night and winter power | Siting; variability | No fuel; scalable |
| Batteries | −85% since 2010 | Daily shifting; backup | Duration; minerals | Fast response; islanding |
| Mini-grids | Falling | Off-grid communities | Finance; regulation | Local control |
| Clean cooking | Moderate | Household health | Fuel supply; cost | Indoor air; forests |
| Heat pumps | Moderate | Heating and cooling | Retrofit; grid load | Efficiency; fuel-free |
| Solar pumps | Falling | Irrigation | Water tables | Drought resilience |

Two conclusions follow. First, the energy transition is now primarily an integration and infrastructure challenge rather than a cost challenge: the technologies are cheap enough, but grids, storage, permitting and supply chains determine how fast they spread. Second, decentralised technologies have a specific resilience value that centralised systems cannot match, because they allow communities to keep essential services running when the grid fails.

What would change these conclusions? A breakthrough in long-duration storage would reduce the need for backup generation and make high-renewable grids cheaper. A rapid expansion of grid investment in low-income countries would unlock demand that is currently constrained by unreliable supply. And a sustained decline in battery and mineral costs would make electrification of transport and heating faster. The most important variable remains the pace of policy and investment, because the technology is already available and the cost curve is already falling.

# Chapter 20: Climate Psychology and Mental Health

*Theme: climate anxiety, trauma and building psychological resilience*

What follows concerns climate anxiety, trauma and building psychological resilience. The chapter separates observation from projection, and projection from speculation, because the three carry different obligations for anyone making a decision. Readers who disagree with the conclusion should be able to see exactly where.

## 20.1 Climate Anxiety and Rational Alarm

Kristie Ebi (2018) puts it plainly: if you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is — people are suffering and dying right now; risks rise without urgent investment in health systems.

Ebi's statement comes from her IPCC SR15 lead authorship and long-term research at the University of Washington CHanGE on heat and health. 'If you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is' is her analogy for clear risk communication — understating severity delays life-saving action. 'People are suffering and dying right now' counters the view that climate health impacts are future, citing documented rises in heat-related illness and mortality among outdoor workers, elders, and low-income groups. 'Risks rise without urgent investment in health systems' points to the need for heat early warning, expanded primary care, and climate-resilient facilities. Ebi stresses that health evidence is actionable and economically justified, with co-benefits for productivity and equity. For climate anxiety and rational alarm within climate anxiety, her framing treats adaptation as public health preparedness, requiring surveillance, workforce training, and funding that reaches the front lines. For climate anxiety and rational alarm, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

Most disagreements about climate anxiety and rational alarm are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Within mental-health services and schools, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

The evidence base behind climate anxiety and rational alarm is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). In the work of mental-health services and schools, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

Uncertainty in climate anxiety and rational alarm is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). For mental-health services and schools, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). Set beside the record, the episode indicates direction without fixing the rate. Within mental-health services and schools, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

It is worth separating what would change the conclusion from what would merely add detail. A revision of the observed trend, or evidence that adaptation is failing faster than expected, would matter; another confirmation of the same direction would not. That test keeps attention on the claims that carry weight. Within mental-health services and schools, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 20.2 Youth Anxiety and Government Inaction

As Susan Clayton (2020) argues, climate anxiety is a rational alarm, especially for youth sensing betrayal by elders.

Clayton argues that climate anxiety is a rational response to a real threat rather than a disorder. Distress concentrates among people who trust the science and feel unheard by institutions. Clinical framing and political framing therefore point in different directions. Applied to youth anxiety and government inaction, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The evidence base for youth anxiety and government inaction is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). In the work of mental-health services and schools, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

An honest account of youth anxiety and government inaction distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. For mental-health services and schools, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

Discussion of youth anxiety and government inaction usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Read that way, the question for mental-health services and schools is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. Within mental-health services and schools, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Taken together, the mechanism, the observed record and the institutional response describe a risk that is measurable but not fixed. The practical question is not whether the trend is real, but which decisions today remain defensible across the full range of projections — and those are usually the decisions that are cheap to reverse. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 20.3 Trauma, PTSD and Mental Health Impacts

As Kristie Ebi (2018) argues, if you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is — people are suffering and dying right now; risks rise without urgent investment in health systems.

Ebi's statement comes from her IPCC SR15 lead authorship and long-term research at the University of Washington CHanGE on heat and health. 'If you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is' is her analogy for clear risk communication — understating severity delays life-saving action. 'People are suffering and dying right now' counters the view that climate health impacts are future, citing documented rises in heat-related illness and mortality among outdoor workers, elders, and low-income groups. 'Risks rise without urgent investment in health systems' points to the need for heat early warning, expanded primary care, and climate-resilient facilities. Ebi stresses that health evidence is actionable and economically justified, with co-benefits for productivity and equity. For trauma within climate anxiety, her framing treats adaptation as public health preparedness, requiring surveillance, workforce training, and funding that reaches the front lines. For trauma, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). In the work of mental-health services and schools, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

Not all statements about trauma, PTSD and mental health impacts carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. For mental-health services and schools, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and trauma, PTSD and mental health impacts is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Within mental-health services and schools, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

The record on trauma, PTSD and mental health impacts is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how mental-health services and schools should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, the practical question is what would have to change first.

A recent marker points the same way: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. In the work of mental-health services and schools, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Certainty is unevenly distributed across this literature. Detection is firm, projection is conditional, and cost estimates are contingent on choices not yet made. A reader who wants a single number will be disappointed; a reader who wants a defensible decision has what is needed. In the work of mental-health services and schools, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 20.4 Building Psychological Resilience

Susan Clayton (2020) puts it plainly: climate anxiety is a rational alarm, especially for youth sensing betrayal by elders.

Clayton argues that climate anxiety is a rational response to a real threat rather than a disorder. Distress concentrates among people who trust the science and feel unheard by institutions. Clinical framing and political framing therefore point in different directions. Read against building psychological resilience, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The strength of the claim about building psychological resilience varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. For mental-health services and schools, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

It helps to ask what question building psychological resilience is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Within mental-health services and schools, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on building psychological resilience rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of mental-health services and schools, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). It matters less as a record than as evidence that the baseline has already moved. For mental-health services and schools, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

What emerges is less a prediction than a test. A measure is adequate if it still works when the upper end of the range arrives; a plan is credible if it names who acts, with what authority and with what money. Judged that way, the gap between what is known and what is done is not a knowledge gap. For mental-health services and schools, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 20.5 Case Study: Climate Psychology and Mental Health

A single episode illustrates the mechanism: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). Read closely, it is a record of thresholds crossed, capacities exhausted and responsibilities disputed — the same elements that recur, at different scales, in every chapter of this book. For mental-health services and schools, the episode fixes the date at which the argument stops being hypothetical.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). Within mental-health services and schools, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

What travels to other settings is not the specific hazard but the pattern of who was warned, who could move and who was compensated.



## 20.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Climate Psychology and Mental Health

For climate anxiety and mental health, the near term is emotional: by 2040, 35–45% of teenagers report weekly climate anxiety, heat and displacement raise depression 15–25%, and post-flood trauma lasts 6–12 months without support.

By 2050, chronic stress becomes a public-health cost in hot and insecure regions, and by 2100 a high-emissions path doubles chronic stress burdens where heat and displacement persist (Watts et al., 2021; WHO, 2023).

Peer groups, green spaces, counseling in schools and clinics, and action projects that restore a sense of agency turn anxiety into engagement.

[[TABLE:Table 20.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate anxiety, trauma and building psychological resilience (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | anxiety 35–45% of teens weekly |
| 2050 | chronic stress raises health costs |
| 2100 | chronic burden ×2 in hot, insecure zones |

Watch school counseling referrals, sleep loss during heatwaves, and community participation in recovery projects; mental-health trends are medium-confidence, but local service demand is visible.
